People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi, doctor. Yeah, thank you for seeing me. Um, so, I guess the main reason I'm here is... well, I'm a secondary school teacher, and the workload outside of actual teaching hours is pretty heavy. I really care about my job and my students, but I've been having this massive issue just... getting through the necessary paperwork. 

Like, I came in because I can sit down at my desk at home with a huge stack of student essays, or maybe a massive curriculum document from the district that I absolutely have to review for the next term. I'll sit there, fully intending to work. I've got my coffee, my grading pens, I've cleared my desk—I'm totally ready to go. But then, I don't know, within ten minutes or so, I'll suddenly snap to and realize I’ve just been staring at the exact same page the whole time. 

My eyes are physically on the paper, but I'm thinking about three other completely different things—like an email I forgot to send to a parent, or a conversation I had in the staff room earlier, or what I need to pick up from the grocery store. I'm barely taking in a single word of what’s actually in front of me. And then I have to re-read it, and the same thing happens all over again. It's just incredibly frustrating because it turns a simple one-hour grading session into this whole exhausting evening ordeal, and I just can't seem to keep my mind on the page no matter how hard I try to force it. It's really starting to wear me down.
=====================================

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, worrying? No, I wouldn't really say I'm a worrier, honestly. Like, when I said my mind wanders to things like the grocery list or that email I forgot, it's not because I'm actively stressing out about them. It's not this heavy, anxious feeling in my chest or anything like that. I mean, sure, if I'm about to miss a hard deadline for midterm grades, I'll get stressed, but who wouldn't, right? Teaching is a stressful job just by nature. But in general, no, I'm not sitting there agonizing over my health or my finances or the future beyond just the normal day-to-day stuff everyone deals with. 

It's actually kind of the opposite of worrying, in a weird way? It's really just... noise. Like my brain is flipping through channels on a TV and someone else has the remote. When I'm thinking about that conversation in the staff room, for example, I'm not overanalyzing what I said or worrying that the other teacher is mad at me. I'm literally just replaying it, or maybe I'm suddenly thinking about the brand of coffee the history teacher was drinking during that conversation, and then wondering where he bought it, and then thinking about how I need to buy coffee, and whether I should try a dark roast this time. 

So it's not anxiety at all. It's just a constant stream of random, mostly harmless thoughts. I just can't seem to keep the channel locked on the one show I'm actually supposed to be watching, which is the stack of essays right in front of my face. But yeah, to answer your question, no, I don't have that excessive worry thing. I don't lie awake terrified of things going wrong. It's really just the frustration of not being able to direct my own focus when I need to.

I'll sit there, fully intending to work. I've got my coffee, my grading pens, I've cleared my desk—I'm totally ready to go. But then, I don't know, within ten minutes or so, I'll suddenly snap to and realize I’ve just been staring at the exact same page the whole time. \n\nMy eyes are physically on the paper, but I'm thinking about three other completely different things—like an email I forgot to send to a parent, or a conversation I had in the staff room earlier, or what I need to pick up from the grocery store. I'm barely taking in a single word of what’s actually in front of me. And then I have to re-read it, and the same thing happens all over again. It's just incredibly frustrating because it turns a simple one-hour grading session into this whole exhausting evening ordeal, and I just can't seem to keep my mind on the page no matter how hard I try to force it. It's really starting to wear me down.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about things beyond just your workload?\n\nPatient: Um, worrying? No, I wouldn't really say I'm a worrier, honestly. Like, when I said my mind wanders to things like the grocery list or that email I forgot, it's not because I'm actively stressing out about them. It's not this heavy, anxious feeling in my chest or anything like that. I mean, sure, if I'm about to miss a hard deadline for midterm grades, I'll get stressed, but who wouldn't, right? Teaching is a stressful job just by nature. But in general, no, I'm not sitting there agonizing over my health or my finances or the future beyond just the normal day-to-day stuff everyone deals with. \n\nIt's actually kind of the opposite of worrying, in a weird way? It's really just... noise. Like my brain is flipping through channels on a TV and someone else has the remote. When I'm thinking about that conversation in the staff room, for example, I'm not overanalyzing what I said or worrying that the other teacher is mad at me. I'm literally just replaying it, or maybe I'm suddenly thinking about the brand of coffee the history teacher was drinking during that conversation, and then wondering where he bought it, and then thinking about how I need to buy coffee, and whether I should try a dark roast this time. \n\nSo it's not anxiety at all. It's just a constant stream of random, mostly harmless thoughts. I just can't seem to keep the channel locked on the one show I'm actually supposed to be watching, which is the stack of essays right in front of my face. But yeah, to answer your question, no, I don't have that excessive worry thing. I don't lie awake terrified of things going wrong. Um, well, no, it’s definitely not just the grading, though I guess that’s the most punishing part of it because of the deadlines and the sheer volume of it. But if I really step back and look at it... yeah, it bleeds into pretty much everything else, too. Even things I actually want to be doing, which is the crazy part. 

Like, for instance, last weekend I sat down to watch this movie. It was this big sci-fi thing I’d been looking forward to seeing for literally months, right? I got the popcorn, I’m on the couch, totally relaxed. And I’m watching the screen, but then, I don't know, I notice the lamp in the corner of the living room is flickering just a tiny bit. And then I start wondering if it’s the bulb going out or if it's the wiring in this old apartment, and then that makes me remember I need to email the landlord about the slow drain in the bathroom sink, and then I'm mentally drafting that email... 

And before I know it, like, fifteen or twenty minutes of the movie have completely gone by. The characters are suddenly in a totally different location, someone is injured, and I have absolutely zero idea how they got there. I literally have to grab the remote and rewind it, sometimes two or three times for the exact same scene just to absorb what they're actually saying. 

And it happens in professional settings besides the paperwork, too. Like in staff meetings. The principal will be standing right there at the front, going over something really important, like the new lockdown protocols. And I need to know that stuff, obviously, because I’m responsible for thirty teenagers if something goes wrong. But I’ll be staring right at him, making perfect eye contact, nodding my head so it looks like I'm engaged, and my brain is just... completely gone. I'm trying to remember if I left my travel mug in the staff room or if it's still in my car, and I'm mentally retracing my steps from the parking lot. It’s honestly so embarrassing, especially if someone actually asks me a question and I snap back to reality and realize I haven't heard a single word anyone has said for the last ten minutes. So, yeah, it's definitely not just the paperwork. It's kind of just... everywhere. It's just that the paperwork is where it really, really impacts my life the most, you know?

It’s honestly so embarrassing, especially if someone actually asks me a question and I snap back to reality and realize I haven't heard a single word anyone has said for the last ten minutes. So, yeah, it's definitely not just the paperwork. It's kind of just... everywhere. It's just that the paperwork is where it really, really impacts my life the most, you know?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, interrupting people? Blurting things out? No, I wouldn't say I do that. Honestly, I'm usually the quiet one in conversations. I'm definitely not the guy who jumps in and talks over people or finishes their sentences. I mean, if anything, because my mind is usually wandering off somewhere else, I'm actually a little slower to respond when someone talks to me. 

Like, if I'm in the staff room and the science teacher, Dave—he's a great guy, really chatty, always talking about his weekend fishing trips or whatever—if he's telling me a story, I'm usually just nodding along. But like I said before, I might be thinking about a lesson plan or wondering if I remembered to lock my classroom door. So when he finally finishes his story and pauses for me to say something, there's usually this awkward beat where I have to kind of scramble to pull myself back into the moment and figure out what a normal response would be based on the last three words I actually heard him say. 

I'm definitely not jumping the gun and blurting things out. I'm usually just trying to catch up to where the conversation is at. And in the classroom, too, with the kids, I'm pretty patient. I let them finish their thoughts, even when they're rambling on about something totally unrelated to the lesson, which teenagers do all the time. I don't cut them off. So, yeah, no, the blurting things out or interrupting... that's not really something I struggle with. It's more just the zoning out part that gets me in trouble.

It's really starting to wear me down.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about things beyond just your workload?\n\nPatient: Um, worrying? No, I wouldn't really say I'm a worrier, honestly. Like, when I said my mind wanders to things like the grocery list or that email I forgot, it's not because I'm actively stressing out about them. It's not this heavy, anxious feeling in my chest or anything like that. I mean, sure, if I'm about to miss a hard deadline for midterm grades, I'll get stressed, but who wouldn't, right? Teaching is a stressful job just by nature. But in general, no, I'm not sitting there agonizing over my health or my finances or the future beyond just the normal day-to-day stuff everyone deals with. \n\nIt's actually kind of the opposite of worrying, in a weird way? It's really just... noise. Like my brain is flipping through channels on a TV and someone else has the remote. When I'm thinking about that conversation in the staff room, for example, I'm not overanalyzing what I said or worrying that the other teacher is mad at me. I'm literally just replaying it, or maybe I'm suddenly thinking about the brand of coffee the history teacher was drinking during that conversation, and then wondering where he bought it, and then thinking about how I need to buy coffee, and whether I should try a dark roast this time. \n\nSo it's not anxiety at all. It's just a constant stream of random, mostly harmless thoughts. I just can't seem to keep the channel locked on the one show I'm actually supposed to be watching, which is the stack of essays right in front of my face. But yeah, to answer your question, no, I don't have that excessive worry thing. I don't lie awake terrified of things going wrong. It's really just the frustration of not being able to direct my own focus when I need to.\n\nDoctor: Does this difficulty with focusing happen only when you're doing paperwork or grading, or do you find it hard to concentrate in other situations as well?\n\nPatient: Um, well, no, it’s definitely not just the grading, though I guess that’s the most punishing part of it because of the deadlines and the sheer volume of it. But if I really step back and look at it... yeah, it bleeds into pretty much everything else, too. Even things I actually want to be doing, which is the crazy part. \n\nLike, for instance, last weekend I sat down to watch this movie. It was this big sci-fi thing I’d been looking forward to seeing for literally months, right? I got the popcorn, I’m on the couch, totally relaxed. And I’m watching the screen, but then, I don't know, I notice the lamp in the corner of the living room is flickering just a tiny bit. And then I start wondering if it’s the bulb going out or if it's the wiring in this old apartment, and then that makes me remember I need to email the landlord about the slow drain in the bathroom sink, and then I'm mentally drafting that email... \n\nAnd before I know it, like, fifteen or twenty minutes of the movie have completely gone by. The characters are suddenly in a totally different location, someone is injured, and I have absolutely zero idea how they got there. I literally have to grab the remote and rewind it, sometimes two or three times for the exact same scene just to absorb what they're actually saying. \n\nAnd it happens in professional settings besides the paperwork, too. Like in staff meetings. The principal will be standing right there at the front, going over something really important, like the new lockdown protocols. And I need to know that stuff, obviously, because I’m responsible for thirty teenagers if something goes wrong. But I’ll be staring right at him, making perfect eye contact, nodding my head so it looks like I'm engaged, and my brain is just... completely gone. I'm trying to remember if I left my travel mug in the staff room or if it's still in my car, and I'm mentally retracing my steps from the parking lot. It’s honestly so embarrassing, especially if someone actually asks me a question and I snap back to reality and realize I haven't heard a single word anyone has said for the last ten minutes. So, yeah, it's definitely not just the paperwork. It's kind of just... everywhere. It's just that the paperwork is where it really, really impacts my life the most, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others or blurting out answers before they've finished speaking?\n\nPatient: Um, interrupting people? Blurting things out? No, I wouldn't say I do that. Honestly, I'm usually the quiet one in conversations. I'm definitely not the guy who jumps in and talks over people or finishes their sentences. I mean, if anything, because my mind is usually wandering off somewhere else, I'm actually a little slower to respond when someone talks to me. \n\nLike, if I'm in the staff room and the science teacher, Dave—he's a great guy, really chatty, always talking about his weekend fishing trips or whatever—if he's telling me a story, I'm usually just nodding along. But like I said before, I might be thinking about a lesson plan or wondering if I remembered to lock my classroom door. So when he finally finishes his story and pauses for me to say something, there's usually this awkward beat where I have to kind of scramble to pull myself back into the moment and figure out what a normal response would be based on the last three words I actually heard him say. \n\nI'm definitely not jumping the gun and blurting things out. I'm usually just trying to catch up to where the conversation is at. And in the classroom, too, with the kids, I'm pretty patient. I let them finish their thoughts, even when they're rambling on about something totally unrelated to the lesson, which teenagers do all the time. I don't cut them off. So, yeah, no, the blurting things out or interrupting... that's not really something I struggle with. It's more just the zoning out part that gets me in trouble.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I let them finish their thoughts, even when they're rambling on about something totally unrelated to the lesson, which teenagers do all the time. I don't cut them off. So, yeah, no, the blurting things out or interrupting... that's not really something I struggle with. It's more just the zoning out part that gets me in trouble.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you have trouble organizing your tasks or managing your time effectively?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 31 / secondary_school_teacher / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, organizing tasks and time management? Yeah, that is... that's a massive issue for me. It's honestly probably the second biggest problem next to the grading thing. I mean, they kind of feed into each other, really. But just in general, trying to organize my day or figure out how long something is going to take me is like... it's like trying to nail Jell-O to a wall. 

Like, okay, take my prep period, for example. I have 45 minutes every day, right? Fourth period. And in my head, I’ll tell myself, "Okay, today I am going to use this 45 minutes to photocopy the history quizzes for tomorrow, email Sarah’s parents about her missing assignments, and finally put up that new bulletin board display." Which, if you think about it, is a completely reasonable list for 45 minutes. Any other teacher could knock that out in half an hour and have time for a coffee. 

But what actually happens is, I walk into the workroom to make the copies, and I see someone left a jam in machine two. So I spend ten minutes trying to fix the jam, because I can't just leave it, right? And while I'm doing that, I remember I was supposed to ask the English department head about a textbook order. So I leave the copies—unmade—and walk down the hall to her room, but she's teaching, so I can't interrupt. On my way back, I stop at the bathroom, and then I realize I'm thirsty, so I go to the staff room for water. While I'm getting water, I see a flyer on the fridge about a staff potluck, and I spend five minutes standing there trying to decide if I should bring potato salad or just buy some cookies. 

And then the bell rings. The 45 minutes are completely gone. I haven't made a single copy, I haven't emailed Sarah's parents, and the bulletin board stuff is still sitting in a pile on my desk. And this happens to me constantly. I just... I have no internal clock. I think something will take five minutes and it takes an hour, or I think I have plenty of time before a meeting and suddenly I'm sprinting down the hallway because I'm ten minutes late. 

My desk is another story entirely. It's just... it's a disaster zone. I try, I really do. I'll buy those little organizers from Target, the colorful folders, the trays... but they just end up becoming part of the mess. I'll have a stack of ungraded papers mixed in with permission slips from three weeks ago, a half-empty water bottle, a random assortment of pens—half of which don't even work—and sticky notes everywhere. Oh my god, the sticky notes. I write things down so I won't forget them, but then I just have a desk covered in neon squares, and I don't even look at them anymore because they just blend into the background noise of the mess. 

So when it comes to prioritizing, it's just a total wash. Everything feels equally urgent, or nothing feels urgent until it's an absolute crisis. Like, I won't start putting grades into the system until the portal is literally closing in two hours, and then I'm in this blind panic, sweating, typing furiously, trying to decipher my own messy handwriting in my grade book. It's exhausting. It's like I'm constantly treading water just to keep from drowning in my own disorganization, and I'm watching all the other teachers just casually swimming laps around me. So, yeah, time management and organizing... it's a huge, huge struggle for me.
=====================================

I'll sit there, fully intending to work. I've got my coffee, my grading pens, I've cleared my desk—I'm totally ready to go. But then, I don't know, within ten minutes or so, I'll suddenly snap to and realize I’ve just been staring at the exact same page the whole time. \n\nMy eyes are physically on the paper, but I'm thinking about three other completely different things—like an email I forgot to send to a parent, or a conversation I had in the staff room earlier, or what I need to pick up from the grocery store. Teaching is a stressful job just by nature. It's really just... noise. Like my brain is flipping through channels on a TV and someone else has the remote. When I'm thinking about that conversation in the staff room, for example, I'm not overanalyzing what I said or worrying that the other teacher is mad at me. I'm literally just replaying it, or maybe I'm suddenly thinking about the brand of coffee the history teacher was drinking during that conversation, and then wondering where he bought it, and then thinking about how I need to buy coffee, and whether I should try a dark roast this time. \n\nSo it's not anxiety at all. It's just a constant stream of random, mostly harmless thoughts. I just can't seem to keep the channel locked on the one show I'm actually supposed to be watching, which is the stack of essays right in front of my face. But yeah, to answer your question, no, I don't have that excessive worry thing. I don't lie awake terrified of things going wrong. It's really just the frustration of not being able to direct my own focus when I need to.\n\nDoctor: Does this difficulty with focusing happen only when you're doing paperwork or grading, or do you find it hard to concentrate in other situations as well?\n\nPatient: Um, well, no, it’s definitely not just the grading, though I guess that’s the most punishing part of it because of the deadlines and the sheer volume of it. The characters are suddenly in a totally different location, someone is injured, and I have absolutely zero idea how they got there. I literally have to grab the remote and rewind it, sometimes two or three times for the exact same scene just to absorb what they're actually saying. \n\nAnd it happens in professional settings besides the paperwork, too. But if I really step back and look at it... yeah, it bleeds into pretty much everything else, too. Even things I actually want to be doing, which is the crazy part. \n\nLike, for instance, last weekend I sat down to watch this movie. And I’m watching the screen, but then, I don't know, I notice the lamp in the corner of the living room is flickering just a tiny bit. And then I start wondering if it’s the bulb going out or if it's the wiring in this old apartment, and then that makes me remember I need to email the landlord about the slow drain in the bathroom sink, and then I'm mentally drafting that email... \n\nAnd before I know it, like, fifteen or twenty minutes of the movie have completely gone by. The characters are suddenly in a totally different location, someone is injured, and I have absolutely zero idea how they got there. I literally have to grab the remote and rewind it, sometimes two or three times for the exact same scene just to absorb what they're actually saying. \n\nAnd it happens in professional settings besides the paperwork, too. Like in staff meetings. The principal will be standing right there at the front, going over something really important, like the new lockdown protocols. And I need to know that stuff, obviously, because I’m responsible for thirty teenagers if something goes wrong. But I’ll be staring right at him, making perfect eye contact, nodding my head so it looks like I'm engaged, and my brain is just... completely gone. I'm trying to remember if I left my travel mug in the staff room or if it's still in my car, and I'm mentally retracing my steps from the parking lot. It’s honestly so embarrassing, especially if someone actually asks me a question and I snap back to reality and realize I haven't heard a single word anyone has said for the last ten minutes. So, yeah, it's definitely not just the paperwork. It's kind of just... everywhere. It's just that the paperwork is where it really, really impacts my life the most, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others or blurting out answers before they've finished speaking?\n\nPatient: Um, interrupting people? Blurting things out? No, I wouldn't say I do that. Honestly, I'm usually the quiet one in conversations. I'm definitely not the guy who jumps in and talks over people or finishes their sentences. I mean, if anything, because my mind is usually wandering off somewhere else, I'm actually a little slower to respond when someone talks to me. \n\nLike, if I'm in the staff room and the science teacher, Dave—he's a great guy, really chatty, always talking about his weekend fishing trips or whatever—if he's telling me a story, I'm usually just nodding along. But like I said before, I might be thinking about a lesson plan or wondering if I remembered to lock my classroom door. So when he finally finishes his story and pauses for me to say something, there's usually this awkward beat where I have to kind of scramble to pull myself back into the moment and figure out what a normal response would be based on the last three words I actually heard him say. \n\nI'm definitely not jumping the gun and blurting things out. I'm usually just trying to catch up to where the conversation is at. And in the classroom, too, with the kids, I'm pretty patient. I let them finish their thoughts, even when they're rambling on about something totally unrelated to the lesson, which teenagers do all the time. I don't cut them off. So, yeah, no, the blurting things out or interrupting... that's not really something I struggle with. It's more just the zoning out part that gets me in trouble.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your tasks or managing your time effectively?\n\nPatient: Um, organizing tasks and time management? Yeah, that is... that's a massive issue for me. It's honestly probably the second biggest problem next to the grading thing. I mean, they kind of feed into each other, really. But just in general, trying to organize my day or figure out how long something is going to take me is like... it's like trying to nail Jell-O to a wall. \n\nLike, okay, take my prep period, for example. I have 45 minutes every day, right? Fourth period. And in my head, I’ll tell myself, \"Okay, today I am going to use this 45 minutes to photocopy the history quizzes for tomorrow, email Sarah’s parents about her missing assignments, and finally put up that new bulletin board display.\" Which, if you think about it, is a completely reasonable list for 45 minutes. Any other teacher could knock that out in half an hour and have time for a coffee. \n\nBut what actually happens is, I walk into the workroom to make the copies, and I see someone left a jam in machine two. So I spend ten minutes trying to fix the jam, because I can't just leave it, right? And while I'm doing that, I remember I was supposed to ask the English department head about a textbook order. So I leave the copies—unmade—and walk down the hall to her room, but she's teaching, so I can't interrupt. On my way back, I stop at the bathroom, and then I realize I'm thirsty, so I go to the staff room for water. While I'm getting water, I see a flyer on the fridge about a staff potluck, and I spend five minutes standing there trying to decide if I should bring potato salad or just buy some cookies. \n\nAnd then the bell rings. The 45 minutes are completely gone. I haven't made a single copy, I haven't emailed Sarah's parents, and the bulletin board stuff is still sitting in a pile on my desk. And this happens to me constantly. I just... I have no internal clock. I think something will take five minutes and it takes an hour, or I think I have plenty of time before a meeting and suddenly I'm sprinting down the hallway because I'm ten minutes late. \n\nMy desk is another story entirely. It's just... it's a disaster zone. I try, I really do. I'll buy those little organizers from Target, the colorful folders, the trays... but they just end up becoming part of the mess. I'll have a stack of ungraded papers mixed in with permission slips from three weeks ago, a half-empty water bottle, a random assortment of pens—half of which don't even work—and sticky notes everywhere. Oh my god, the sticky notes. I write things down so I won't forget them, but then I just have a desk covered in neon squares, and I don't even look at them anymore because they just blend into the background noise of the mess. \n\nSo when it comes to prioritizing, it's just a total wash. Everything feels equally urgent, or nothing feels urgent until it's an absolute crisis. Like, I won't start putting grades into the system until the portal is literally closing in two hours, and then I'm in this blind panic, sweating, typing furiously, trying to decipher my own messy handwriting in my grade book. It's exhausting. It's like I'm constantly treading water just to keep from drowning in my own disorganization, and I'm watching all the other teachers just casually swimming laps around me. So, yeah, time management and organizing... it's a huge, huge struggle for me.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless, like you need to get up and move around?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, fidgeting? Restless? No, not really. I mean, I know that's what people usually think of when they think of attention issues, right? Like the kid bouncing off the walls or the person who can't stop shaking their leg. But physically? I'm actually pretty stationary. I can sit in one spot for hours on end without really moving a muscle. 

Like, I have this desk chair at home—it's this big, kind of worn-out leather executive chair I got from a thrift store a few years ago. It was actually a total steal, only forty bucks, and it's incredibly comfortable. Anyway, I can sit in that chair for literally four hours straight on a Sunday afternoon. Now, am I actually doing what I'm supposed to be doing while I'm sitting there? No, definitely not. I'm supposed to be grading those history essays or planning out the week's curriculum. But my body isn't moving. I'm not tapping my foot, I'm not pacing the living room, and I never get that feeling like I just need to get up and burn off energy. 

I see that kind of restlessness in my students all the time, though. I have this one kid, Jason, in my third-period class, and he literally cannot keep all four legs of his chair on the floor for more than thirty seconds. He's always drumming his pencils, asking to go to the bathroom just so he can walk the halls, shifting his weight around. I totally get what that looks like because I have to manage it every single day in the classroom. But for me? I just don't have that physical motor running. 

If anything, the restlessness is entirely in my head. My brain is the thing that's bouncing around like a pinball machine, going from the grading to wondering if my car needs an oil change to trying to remember if I bought coffee filters, but my body is just planted right there at the desk. My ex-girlfriend actually used to say I was like a statue when I zoned out. She'd walk into the room and I'd just be completely still, staring at a blank wall or a paused TV screen for twenty minutes. So no, I don't really feel the need to get up and move around at all. I'm usually just stuck wherever I sat down, almost like I'm paralyzed by all the thoughts of everything I'm *not* getting done. So the physical fidgeting isn't really a thing for me.

I'm usually just trying to catch up to where the conversation is at. And in the classroom, too, with the kids, I'm pretty patient. I let them finish their thoughts, even when they're rambling on about something totally unrelated to the lesson, which teenagers do all the time. I don't cut them off. So, yeah, no, the blurting things out or interrupting... that's not really something I struggle with. It's more just the zoning out part that gets me in trouble.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your tasks or managing your time effectively?\n\nPatient: Um, organizing tasks and time management? Yeah, that is... that's a massive issue for me. It's honestly probably the second biggest problem next to the grading thing. I mean, they kind of feed into each other, really. But just in general, trying to organize my day or figure out how long something is going to take me is like... it's like trying to nail Jell-O to a wall. \n\nLike, okay, take my prep period, for example. I have 45 minutes every day, right? Fourth period. And in my head, I’ll tell myself, \"Okay, today I am going to use this 45 minutes to photocopy the history quizzes for tomorrow, email Sarah’s parents about her missing assignments, and finally put up that new bulletin board display.\" Which, if you think about it, is a completely reasonable list for 45 minutes. Any other teacher could knock that out in half an hour and have time for a coffee. \n\nBut what actually happens is, I walk into the workroom to make the copies, and I see someone left a jam in machine two. So I spend ten minutes trying to fix the jam, because I can't just leave it, right? And while I'm doing that, I remember I was supposed to ask the English department head about a textbook order. So I leave the copies—unmade—and walk down the hall to her room, but she's teaching, so I can't interrupt. On my way back, I stop at the bathroom, and then I realize I'm thirsty, so I go to the staff room for water. While I'm getting water, I see a flyer on the fridge about a staff potluck, and I spend five minutes standing there trying to decide if I should bring potato salad or just buy some cookies. \n\nAnd then the bell rings. The 45 minutes are completely gone. I haven't made a single copy, I haven't emailed Sarah's parents, and the bulletin board stuff is still sitting in a pile on my desk. And this happens to me constantly. I just... I have no internal clock. I think something will take five minutes and it takes an hour, or I think I have plenty of time before a meeting and suddenly I'm sprinting down the hallway because I'm ten minutes late. \n\nMy desk is another story entirely. It's just... it's a disaster zone. I try, I really do. I'll buy those little organizers from Target, the colorful folders, the trays... but they just end up becoming part of the mess. I'll have a stack of ungraded papers mixed in with permission slips from three weeks ago, a half-empty water bottle, a random assortment of pens—half of which don't even work—and sticky notes everywhere. Oh my god, the sticky notes. I write things down so I won't forget them, but then I just have a desk covered in neon squares, and I don't even look at them anymore because they just blend into the background noise of the mess. \n\nSo when it comes to prioritizing, it's just a total wash. Everything feels equally urgent, or nothing feels urgent until it's an absolute crisis. Like, I won't start putting grades into the system until the portal is literally closing in two hours, and then I'm in this blind panic, sweating, typing furiously, trying to decipher my own messy handwriting in my grade book. It's exhausting. It's like I'm constantly treading water just to keep from drowning in my own disorganization, and I'm watching all the other teachers just casually swimming laps around me. So, yeah, time management and organizing... it's a huge, huge struggle for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless, like you need to get up and move around?\n\nPatient: Um, fidgeting? Restless? No, not really. I mean, I know that's what people usually think of when they think of attention issues, right? Like the kid bouncing off the walls or the person who can't stop shaking their leg. But physically? I'm actually pretty stationary. I can sit in one spot for hours on end without really moving a muscle. \n\nLike, I have this desk chair at home—it's this big, kind of worn-out leather executive chair I got from a thrift store a few years ago. It was actually a total steal, only forty bucks, and it's incredibly comfortable. Anyway, I can sit in that chair for literally four hours straight on a Sunday afternoon. Now, am I actually doing what I'm supposed to be doing while I'm sitting there? No, definitely not. I'm supposed to be grading those history essays or planning out the week's curriculum. But my body isn't moving. I'm not tapping my foot, I'm not pacing the living room, and I never get that feeling like I just need to get up and burn off energy. \n\nI see that kind of restlessness in my students all the time, though. I have this one kid, Jason, in my third-period class, and he literally cannot keep all four legs of his chair on the floor for more than thirty seconds. He's always drumming his pencils, asking to go to the bathroom just so he can walk the halls, shifting his weight around. I totally get what that looks like because I have to manage it every single day in the classroom. But for me? I just don't have that physical motor running. \n\nIf anything, the restlessness is entirely in my head. My brain is the thing that's bouncing around like a pinball machine, going from the grading to wondering if my car needs an oil change to trying to remember if I bought coffee filters, but my body is just planted right there at the desk. My ex-girlfriend actually used to say I was like a statue when I zoned out. She'd walk into the room and I'd just be completely still, staring at a blank wall or a paused TV screen for twenty minutes. So no, I don't really feel the need to get up and move around at all. I'm usually just stuck wherever I sat down, almost like I'm paralyzed by all the thoughts of everything I'm *not* getting done. So the physical fidgeting isn't really a thing for me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I think something will take five minutes and it takes an hour, or I think I have plenty of time before a meeting and suddenly I'm sprinting down the hallway because I'm ten minutes late. \n\nMy desk is another story entirely. Um, controlling my emotions? No, I wouldn't say that's an issue for me. I don't really get angry or fly off the handle, even when things are super stressful. Honestly, people usually tell me I'm almost too laid back. Which, you know, might just be because I'm totally zoned out half the time like I mentioned before, but still. I'm definitely not the type to yell or have a meltdown. 

I mean, you kind of have to be pretty steady when you teach high school, right? Teenagers will test literally every nerve you have in your body. Just the other day, actually, I had this situation with the school's Wi-Fi. We were doing this big online interactive history module that I had spent—well, I was going to say three hours setting up, but it was probably three hours of me trying to set it up while getting distracted by other tabs, but you know what I mean. Anyway, it was a big deal, and the principal, Mr. Harrison, was actually sitting in the back of the room doing my formal observation for the semester. And of course, the exact moment the kids go to log in, the entire network crashes. Just totally goes down. 

Now, I know some teachers who would completely panic in that situation, right? Or they'd get visibly frustrated, maybe start snapping at the kids who were obviously taking the opportunity to immediately start goofing off and talking loudly. But I didn't do any of that. I didn't get mad or upset. I just kind of sighed, walked over to the whiteboard, and switched gears to a backup discussion about the Cold War. I didn't yell, I didn't get snippy with Mr. Harrison, I didn't have a breakdown. 

Internally, was I stressed? Sure, a little bit, because it's an observation and you want things to go perfectly. But I never lash out. I don't have mood swings or anything like that. When I get really overwhelmed by the grading or the absolute mess on my desk that we talked about, I don't throw things or get angry. I just kind of... deflate, I guess? I get quiet and just sort of stare at it all. So, yeah, the emotional control thing... I don't think I have a problem with that at all. I'm pretty steady. Almost to a fault, really. My mom actually used to say that to me when I was younger, she'd be like, "Are you even reacting to this?" when something stressful happened. I just internalize it, I guess. But yeah, no outbursts or losing my temper. That's really not me.

It's just incredibly frustrating because it turns a simple one-hour grading session into this whole exhausting evening ordeal, and I just can't seem to keep my mind on the page no matter how hard I try to force it. It's really starting to wear me down.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about things beyond just your workload?\n\nPatient: Um, worrying? I'm literally just replaying it, or maybe I'm suddenly thinking about the brand of coffee the history teacher was drinking during that conversation, and then wondering where he bought it, and then thinking about how I need to buy coffee, and whether I should try a dark roast this time. \n\nSo it's not anxiety at all. It's just a constant stream of random, mostly harmless thoughts. I just can't seem to keep the channel locked on the one show I'm actually supposed to be watching, which is the stack of essays right in front of my face. But yeah, to answer your question, no, I don't have that excessive worry thing. I don't lie awake terrified of things going wrong. It's really just the frustration of not being able to direct my own focus when I need to.\n\nDoctor: Does this difficulty with focusing happen only when you're doing paperwork or grading, or do you find it hard to concentrate in other situations as well?\n\nPatient: Um, well, no, it’s definitely not just the grading, though I guess that’s the most punishing part of it because of the deadlines and the sheer volume of it. But if I really step back and look at it... yeah, it bleeds into pretty much everything else, too. Even things I actually want to be doing, which is the crazy part. \n\nLike, for instance, last weekend I sat down to watch this movie. It was this big sci-fi thing I’d been looking forward to seeing for literally months, right? I got the popcorn, I’m on the couch, totally relaxed. And I’m watching the screen, but then, I don't know, I notice the lamp in the corner of the living room is flickering just a tiny bit. And then I start wondering if it’s the bulb going out or if it's the wiring in this old apartment, and then that makes me remember I need to email the landlord about the slow drain in the bathroom sink, and then I'm mentally drafting that email... \n\nAnd before I know it, like, fifteen or twenty minutes of the movie have completely gone by. The characters are suddenly in a totally different location, someone is injured, and I have absolutely zero idea how they got there. I literally have to grab the remote and rewind it, sometimes two or three times for the exact same scene just to absorb what they're actually saying. \n\nAnd it happens in professional settings besides the paperwork, too. Like in staff meetings. The principal will be standing right there at the front, going over something really important, like the new lockdown protocols. And I need to know that stuff, obviously, because I’m responsible for thirty teenagers if something goes wrong. But I’ll be staring right at him, making perfect eye contact, nodding my head so it looks like I'm engaged, and my brain is just... completely gone. I'm trying to remember if I left my travel mug in the staff room or if it's still in my car, and I'm mentally retracing my steps from the parking lot. It’s honestly so embarrassing, especially if someone actually asks me a question and I snap back to reality and realize I haven't heard a single word anyone has said for the last ten minutes. So, yeah, it's definitely not just the paperwork. It's kind of just... everywhere. Blurting things out? No, I wouldn't say I do that. Honestly, I'm usually the quiet one in conversations. I'm definitely not the guy who jumps in and talks over people or finishes their sentences. I mean, if anything, because my mind is usually wandering off somewhere else, I'm actually a little slower to respond when someone talks to me. \n\nLike, if I'm in the staff room and the science teacher, Dave—he's a great guy, really chatty, always talking about his weekend fishing trips or whatever—if he's telling me a story, I'm usually just nodding along. But like I said before, I might be thinking about a lesson plan or wondering if I remembered to lock my classroom door. So when he finally finishes his story and pauses for me to say something, there's usually this awkward beat where I have to kind of scramble to pull myself back into the moment and figure out what a normal response would be based on the last three words I actually heard him say. \n\nI'm definitely not jumping the gun and blurting things out. I'm usually just trying to catch up to where the conversation is at. And in the classroom, too, with the kids, I'm pretty patient. I let them finish their thoughts, even when they're rambling on about something totally unrelated to the lesson, which teenagers do all the time. I don't cut them off. So, yeah, no, the blurting things out or interrupting... that's not really something I struggle with. It's more just the zoning out part that gets me in trouble.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your tasks or managing your time effectively?\n\nPatient: Um, organizing tasks and time management? Yeah, that is... that's a massive issue for me. It's honestly probably the second biggest problem next to the grading thing. I mean, they kind of feed into each other, really. But just in general, trying to organize my day or figure out how long something is going to take me is like... it's like trying to nail Jell-O to a wall. \n\nLike, okay, take my prep period, for example. I have 45 minutes every day, right? Fourth period. And in my head, I’ll tell myself, \"Okay, today I am going to use this 45 minutes to photocopy the history quizzes for tomorrow, email Sarah’s parents about her missing assignments, and finally put up that new bulletin board display.\" Which, if you think about it, is a completely reasonable list for 45 minutes. Any other teacher could knock that out in half an hour and have time for a coffee. \n\nBut what actually happens is, I walk into the workroom to make the copies, and I see someone left a jam in machine two. So I spend ten minutes trying to fix the jam, because I can't just leave it, right? And while I'm doing that, I remember I was supposed to ask the English department head about a textbook order. So I leave the copies—unmade—and walk down the hall to her room, but she's teaching, so I can't interrupt. On my way back, I stop at the bathroom, and then I realize I'm thirsty, so I go to the staff room for water. While I'm getting water, I see a flyer on the fridge about a staff potluck, and I spend five minutes standing there trying to decide if I should bring potato salad or just buy some cookies. \n\nAnd then the bell rings. The 45 minutes are completely gone. I haven't made a single copy, I haven't emailed Sarah's parents, and the bulletin board stuff is still sitting in a pile on my desk. And this happens to me constantly. I just... I have no internal clock. I think something will take five minutes and it takes an hour, or I think I have plenty of time before a meeting and suddenly I'm sprinting down the hallway because I'm ten minutes late. \n\nMy desk is another story entirely. It's just... it's a disaster zone. I try, I really do. I'll buy those little organizers from Target, the colorful folders, the trays... but they just end up becoming part of the mess. I'll have a stack of ungraded papers mixed in with permission slips from three weeks ago, a half-empty water bottle, a random assortment of pens—half of which don't even work—and sticky notes everywhere. Oh my god, the sticky notes. I write things down so I won't forget them, but then I just have a desk covered in neon squares, and I don't even look at them anymore because they just blend into the background noise of the mess. \n\nSo when it comes to prioritizing, it's just a total wash. Everything feels equally urgent, or nothing feels urgent until it's an absolute crisis. Like, I won't start putting grades into the system until the portal is literally closing in two hours, and then I'm in this blind panic, sweating, typing furiously, trying to decipher my own messy handwriting in my grade book. It's exhausting. It's like I'm constantly treading water just to keep from drowning in my own disorganization, and I'm watching all the other teachers just casually swimming laps around me. So, yeah, time management and organizing... it's a huge, huge struggle for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless, like you need to get up and move around?\n\nPatient: Um, fidgeting? Restless? No, not really. I mean, I know that's what people usually think of when they think of attention issues, right? Like the kid bouncing off the walls or the person who can't stop shaking their leg. But physically? I'm actually pretty stationary. I can sit in one spot for hours on end without really moving a muscle. \n\nLike, I have this desk chair at home—it's this big, kind of worn-out leather executive chair I got from a thrift store a few years ago. It was actually a total steal, only forty bucks, and it's incredibly comfortable. Anyway, I can sit in that chair for literally four hours straight on a Sunday afternoon. Now, am I actually doing what I'm supposed to be doing while I'm sitting there? No, definitely not. I'm supposed to be grading those history essays or planning out the week's curriculum. But my body isn't moving. I'm not tapping my foot, I'm not pacing the living room, and I never get that feeling like I just need to get up and burn off energy. \n\nI see that kind of restlessness in my students all the time, though. I have this one kid, Jason, in my third-period class, and he literally cannot keep all four legs of his chair on the floor for more than thirty seconds. He's always drumming his pencils, asking to go to the bathroom just so he can walk the halls, shifting his weight around. I totally get what that looks like because I have to manage it every single day in the classroom. But for me? I just don't have that physical motor running. \n\nIf anything, the restlessness is entirely in my head. My brain is the thing that's bouncing around like a pinball machine, going from the grading to wondering if my car needs an oil change to trying to remember if I bought coffee filters, but my body is just planted right there at the desk. My ex-girlfriend actually used to say I was like a statue when I zoned out. She'd walk into the room and I'd just be completely still, staring at a blank wall or a paused TV screen for twenty minutes. So no, I don't really feel the need to get up and move around at all. I'm usually just stuck wherever I sat down, almost like I'm paralyzed by all the thoughts of everything I'm *not* getting done. So the physical fidgeting isn't really a thing for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble controlling your emotions when you're under stress or frustrated?\n\nPatient: Um, controlling my emotions? No, I wouldn't say that's an issue for me. I don't really get angry or fly off the handle, even when things are super stressful. Honestly, people usually tell me I'm almost too laid back. Which, you know, might just be because I'm totally zoned out half the time like I mentioned before, but still. I'm definitely not the type to yell or have a meltdown. \n\nI mean, you kind of have to be pretty steady when you teach high school, right? Teenagers will test literally every nerve you have in your body. Just the other day, actually, I had this situation with the school's Wi-Fi. We were doing this big online interactive history module that I had spent—well, I was going to say three hours setting up, but it was probably three hours of me trying to set it up while getting distracted by other tabs, but you know what I mean. Anyway, it was a big deal, and the principal, Mr. Harrison, was actually sitting in the back of the room doing my formal observation for the semester. And of course, the exact moment the kids go to log in, the entire network crashes. Just totally goes down. \n\nNow, I know some teachers who would completely panic in that situation, right? Or they'd get visibly frustrated, maybe start snapping at the kids who were obviously taking the opportunity to immediately start goofing off and talking loudly. But I didn't do any of that. I didn't get mad or upset. I just kind of sighed, walked over to the whiteboard, and switched gears to a backup discussion about the Cold War. I didn't yell, I didn't get snippy with Mr. Harrison, I didn't have a breakdown. \n\nInternally, was I stressed? Sure, a little bit, because it's an observation and you want things to go perfectly. But I never lash out. I don't have mood swings or anything like that. When I get really overwhelmed by the grading or the absolute mess on my desk that we talked about, I don't throw things or get angry. I just kind of... deflate, I guess? I get quiet and just sort of stare at it all. So, yeah, the emotional control thing... I don't think I have a problem with that at all. I'm pretty steady. Almost to a fault, really. My mom actually used to say that to me when I was younger, she'd be like, \"Are you even reacting to this?\" when something stressful happened. I just internalize it, I guess. While I'm getting water, I see a flyer on the fridge about a staff potluck, and I spend five minutes standing there trying to decide if I should bring potato salad or just buy some cookies. \n\nAnd then the bell rings. The 45 minutes are completely gone. I haven't made a single copy, I haven't emailed Sarah's parents, and the bulletin board stuff is still sitting in a pile on my desk. And this happens to me constantly. I just... I have no internal clock. I think something will take five minutes and it takes an hour, or I think I have plenty of time before a meeting and suddenly I'm sprinting down the hallway because I'm ten minutes late. \n\nMy desk is another story entirely. It's just... it's a disaster zone. I try, I really do. I'll buy those little organizers from Target, the colorful folders, the trays... but they just end up becoming part of the mess. I'll have a stack of ungraded papers mixed in with permission slips from three weeks ago, a half-empty water bottle, a random assortment of pens—half of which don't even work—and sticky notes everywhere. Oh my god, the sticky notes. I write things down so I won't forget them, but then I just have a desk covered in neon squares, and I don't even look at them anymore because they just blend into the background noise of the mess. \n\nSo when it comes to prioritizing, it's just a total wash. Everything feels equally urgent, or nothing feels urgent until it's an absolute crisis. Like, I won't start putting grades into the system until the portal is literally closing in two hours, and then I'm in this blind panic, sweating, typing furiously, trying to decipher my own messy handwriting in my grade book. It's exhausting. It's like I'm constantly treading water just to keep from drowning in my own disorganization, and I'm watching all the other teachers just casually swimming laps around me. So, yeah, time management and organizing... it's a huge, huge struggle for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless, like you need to get up and move around?\n\nPatient: Um, fidgeting? Restless? No, not really. I mean, I know that's what people usually think of when they think of attention issues, right? Like the kid bouncing off the walls or the person who can't stop shaking their leg. But physically? I'm actually pretty stationary. I can sit in one spot for hours on end without really moving a muscle. \n\nLike, I have this desk chair at home—it's this big, kind of worn-out leather executive chair I got from a thrift store a few years ago. It was actually a total steal, only forty bucks, and it's incredibly comfortable. Anyway, I can sit in that chair for literally four hours straight on a Sunday afternoon. Now, am I actually doing what I'm supposed to be doing while I'm sitting there? No, definitely not. I'm supposed to be grading those history essays or planning out the week's curriculum. But my body isn't moving. I'm not tapping my foot, I'm not pacing the living room, and I never get that feeling like I just need to get up and burn off energy. \n\nI see that kind of restlessness in my students all the time, though. I have this one kid, Jason, in my third-period class, and he literally cannot keep all four legs of his chair on the floor for more than thirty seconds. He's always drumming his pencils, asking to go to the bathroom just so he can walk the halls, shifting his weight around. I totally get what that looks like because I have to manage it every single day in the classroom. But for me? I just don't have that physical motor running. \n\nIf anything, the restlessness is entirely in my head. My brain is the thing that's bouncing around like a pinball machine, going from the grading to wondering if my car needs an oil change to trying to remember if I bought coffee filters, but my body is just planted right there at the desk. My ex-girlfriend actually used to say I was like a statue when I zoned out. She'd walk into the room and I'd just be completely still, staring at a blank wall or a paused TV screen for twenty minutes. So no, I don't really feel the need to get up and move around at all. I'm usually just stuck wherever I sat down, almost like I'm paralyzed by all the thoughts of everything I'm *not* getting done. So the physical fidgeting isn't really a thing for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble controlling your emotions when you're under stress or frustrated?\n\nPatient: Um, controlling my emotions? No, I wouldn't say that's an issue for me. I don't really get angry or fly off the handle, even when things are super stressful. Honestly, people usually tell me I'm almost too laid back. Which, you know, might just be because I'm totally zoned out half the time like I mentioned before, but still. I'm definitely not the type to yell or have a meltdown. \n\nI mean, you kind of have to be pretty steady when you teach high school, right? Teenagers will test literally every nerve you have in your body. Just the other day, actually, I had this situation with the school's Wi-Fi. We were doing this big online interactive history module that I had spent—well, I was going to say three hours setting up, but it was probably three hours of me trying to set it up while getting distracted by other tabs, but you know what I mean. Anyway, it was a big deal, and the principal, Mr. Harrison, was actually sitting in the back of the room doing my formal observation for the semester. And of course, the exact moment the kids go to log in, the entire network crashes. Just totally goes down. \n\nNow, I know some teachers who would completely panic in that situation, right? Or they'd get visibly frustrated, maybe start snapping at the kids who were obviously taking the opportunity to immediately start goofing off and talking loudly. But I didn't do any of that. I didn't get mad or upset. I just kind of sighed, walked over to the whiteboard, and switched gears to a backup discussion about the Cold War. I didn't yell, I didn't get snippy with Mr. Harrison, I didn't have a breakdown. \n\nInternally, was I stressed? Sure, a little bit, because it's an observation and you want things to go perfectly. But I never lash out. I don't have mood swings or anything like that. When I get really overwhelmed by the grading or the absolute mess on my desk that we talked about, I don't throw things or get angry. I just kind of... deflate, I guess? I get quiet and just sort of stare at it all. So, yeah, the emotional control thing... I don't think I have a problem with that at all. I'm pretty steady. Almost to a fault, really. My mom actually used to say that to me when I was younger, she'd be like, \"Are you even reacting to this?\" when something stressful happened. I just internalize it, I guess. But yeah, no outbursts or losing my temper. That's really not me.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often find yourself putting off tasks until the last minute, even when you know they need to be done sooner?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 31 / secondary_school_teacher / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, putting things off... I mean, I know I just talked about the grading thing and doing it right before the portal closes, but I wouldn't really say I put things off, at least not on purpose. I don't sit down and think to myself that I'll just do it tomorrow instead. It's more like... I actually try to start things right away. 

Like, take my lesson plans for next week, for example. I'll tell myself on a Tuesday that I am going to get a head start on next week's unit on the French Revolution. And I will literally sit down at my computer, open up a blank document, and I have every intention of doing it right then and there. I am not putting it off at all. But then I'll realize I need my notes from last year, so I go into my filing cabinet—which, like my desk, is a complete disaster—and while I'm looking for the French Revolution folder, I find a stack of old hall passes and I start organizing those. And then I think about how I should email the main office about getting more of these printed. So I go back to my computer to send the email, but then I see I have a new message from a parent, so I open that to read it. And before I know it, two hours have passed, and I haven't written a single word of the lesson plan. 

So it's not that I decided to put the lesson plan off until Sunday night. I was literally trying to do it on Tuesday. It just didn't happen. So I don't know if that counts as putting things off, because the intention to do it early is absolutely there. I just get completely derailed by everything else. 

My dad actually used to call me a procrastinator when I was in high school, but it always felt so unfair because I would be sitting at my desk in my bedroom for hours trying to do the homework. I wasn't out playing video games or hanging out with friends, I was just stuck in this endless loop of trying to start and getting sidetracked by a million other tiny things. I'd end up organizing my sock drawer or reading the entire Wikipedia page for the band I was listening to instead of doing my math worksheet. So, yeah, I don't really feel like I actively put things off, if that makes sense. I just end up doing them at the last minute by accident because my brain took a giant detour.
