So, yeah, I don't really feel like I actively put things off, if that makes sense. I just end up doing them at the last minute by accident because my brain took a giant detour.\n\nDoctor: Do you often struggle to motivate yourself to start tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: Um, struggle to motivate myself? No, actually, I’d say it’s the exact opposite. I don't have any problem at all getting motivated to start things. If anything, I have too much motivation. I get incredibly fired up about new ideas or projects, like, all the time. \n\nLike, just over winter break, I decided I was going to build a scale model of a World War I trench system for my sophomore classes. I was so incredibly motivated to do it. I mean, I woke up at like 6 AM on a Tuesday, drove all the way to this specific hobby shop two towns over because they had the exact type of high-density foam board I wanted, and I probably spent a hundred and fifty dollars on miniature sandbags, acrylic paint, this tiny little wire to make fake barbed wire... I was just so pumped about it. I came home, cleared off my entire dining room table—well, I shoved all the mail and stuff to one side, anyway—and I was just completely ready to go. The motivation was at like a hundred and ten percent. I literally couldn't wait to dive in. \n\nSo, no, finding the drive to begin isn't the issue at all. I have all the enthusiasm in the world. I’ll sit down with all my supplies, completely eager to get to work. It’s just that... well, kind of like what I was saying before about the lesson plans, right? Once I’m sitting there, I’ll realize I need to look up a historical reference photo of the trench layout to make sure I get the angles right. So I open my laptop, and then I’m looking at photos, but then I see a linked article about the dietary rations of the British infantry, and I start reading that. And then I’m looking up authentic recipes for hardtack, and then I’m in the kitchen checking the pantry to see if I have enough flour to try baking it. \n\nBut the *motivation* to build the trench was absolutely there! I genuinely wanted to do it! I still want to do it, actually. All that foam board and the little sandbags are still sitting right there on my dining table right now, just gathering dust. But it’s never a feeling of, \"Ugh, I don't want to do this,\" or having to drag myself kicking and screaming to start a task. I never sit around feeling unmotivated or apathetic. \n\nActually, my principal, Mr. Harrison—the one who did that observation I mentioned—he always writes on my evaluations that I have \"exceptional enthusiasm and initiative.\" He always points out that I’m the first one to pitch big, ambitious ideas at our department meetings. I’m always the guy saying, \"Hey, what if we completely revamped the civics curriculum to include a mock trial?\" I have so much drive to get these things off the ground. I just... you know, I get lost along the way. But getting that initial spark? Motivating myself to start? No, that’s honestly the easiest part for me. I love starting things.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without fully thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: Um, acting on impulse? No, definitely not. I'm actually the complete opposite of that. I overthink pretty much everything. I never just jump into things without thinking about what's going to happen or what the consequences might be. If anything, I think about the consequences way too much. \n\nLike, okay, take for example when I had to buy a new car last year. My old one—it was this 2008 Honda Civic, great car, honestly, but the transmission was starting to slip, and my mechanic, this guy Dave who I've been going to for years, told me it just wasn't worth the money to fix it anymore. So, I think a truly impulsive person, right, they might just go down to the dealership that weekend, see something shiny that looks nice, sign the papers, and drive off without a second thought. But not me. Oh man, no. I spent... it had to be at least three months researching. \n\nI made these incredibly detailed spreadsheets comparing the gas mileage, the safety ratings, the long-term maintenance costs, the cargo space—because I have to haul a lot of stuff for school, you know, like the foam board I mentioned earlier, or big boxes of textbooks. I was looking at the Toyota RAV4, the Honda CR-V, the Subaru Forester... I must have read every single consumer review on the internet. I was just so worried about making the wrong choice and regretting it later, or buying something that would break down and leave me stranded on the way to work. Which would be a total disaster, because if I'm not there by 7:15 AM, the whole morning duty schedule for the hallway gets messed up and Mr. Harrison has to cover it, which he hates. So I thought through every single possible consequence. I even called my insurance agent, Brenda, to get exact premium quotes on all three models before I even went to a lot to test-drive them. \n\nSo, yeah, I don't just act on impulse. I don't make snap decisions. I mean, even with really little things, I do this. Like if the other teachers in the social studies department want to order lunch on a Friday. They'll be like, \"Hey, we're getting Thai food from that place down the street, what do you want?\" And I will literally sit there staring at the menu for twenty minutes trying to decide if I want Pad Thai or Green Curry, thinking about if the curry will be too heavy and make me sleepy during my 6th-period class, or if the Pad Thai is going to be too messy to eat while I'm trying to grade quizzes. I just don't have that spontaneous, jump-right-in gene. I really don't do anything without completely over-analyzing it first, so acting without thinking is definitely not an issue for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with any other medical or psychological conditions that might be contributing to your difficulties with focus and organization?\n\nPatient: Um, other medical or psychological conditions? No, no, definitely not. I mean, I haven't really been to the doctor much in my life anyway, aside from just the standard stuff. \n\nLike, I go for my routine physicals every year, mostly because our teachers' union negotiated this really good health insurance plan a few years back—which, honestly, was a whole big ordeal with the district, they almost went on strike over the premiums, but anyway, we get these free annual wellness checks now. So I go to my primary care guy, Dr. Evans, and he always says I'm perfectly healthy. He does the whole workup, checks my blood pressure, listens to my heart, and it's always completely fine. I've never had any major health issues, no surgeries, no chronic illnesses or anything like that. I don't have, like, thyroid problems or anything physical that I know of that would mess with my focus. I mean, I guess I don't sleep great sometimes, but that's just because I'm up late trying to finish grading the papers I got distracted from earlier, you know? It's not because of a medical sleep condition. \n\nAnd as for the psychological side of things, no, nothing there either. I've never been diagnosed with depression or anxiety or anything like that. I mean, I get stressed out, sure. Teaching sophomores is just inherently stressful. Especially around standardized testing time in the spring, the whole building just turns into this massive pressure cooker, and I definitely feel the stress then. But it's just normal teacher stress, you know? It's circumstantial. It's not a clinical condition. I've actually never even seen a psychiatrist or a therapist before coming here to talk to you. \n\nSo, yeah, no other diagnoses at all. It's really just always been this... this weird, frustrating struggle with getting sidetracked and being disorganized. And for the longest time, I didn't even think of it as a medical or psychological thing that *could* be diagnosed. I just thought, kind of like what I was saying about my dad calling me a procrastinator, that it was just a personal failing. I thought I just needed to try harder or buy a better planner. \n\nActually, I have an entire drawer in my desk at home just full of old planners. Every single August, right before the school year starts, I go to Staples and I spend like thirty dollars on a really nice, heavy-duty academic planner. I get the nice pens, the color-coded tabs, the whole nine yards, and I tell myself that *this* is the year I'm going to be perfectly organized. And I'll use it religiously for about two weeks, and then I'll forget it in my car one day, and by October it's buried under a pile of ungraded quizzes and I never look at it again. So I always just assumed I was bad at planning, not that I had a condition. So to answer your question, no, I have a completely clean slate medically speaking. Just the focus stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel the need to be active and have trouble engaging in quiet activities?\n\nPatient: Um, feel the need to be active? Trouble with quiet activities? No, not really. Actually, I'd say I'm perfectly fine just sitting still. \n\nI mean, teaching teenagers all day is incredibly exhausting physically. You're constantly on your feet, pacing the front of the room, writing on the board, walking up and down the aisles to make sure nobody is texting under their desks... by the time the final bell rings at 3:15, I am completely wiped out. So when I get home, I honestly just want to sit. I have this big, oversized leather recliner in my living room—it's actually a hand-me-down from my Uncle Pete, and the mechanism is a little squeaky when you pull the lever on the side, but it is just incredibly comfortable. I can sit in that chair for literally hours and not feel any urge whatsoever to get up and move around. \n\nI actually really love quiet activities. I read a lot. Being a history teacher, I tend to gravitate toward these massive, dense biographies. Like, right now, I’m in the middle of this 800-page book on Ulysses S. Grant. It goes into this excruciating detail about his logistics strategies and supply lines during the Vicksburg campaign, which, honestly, most people would probably find incredibly boring and dry. But I can just sit there in total silence for three, maybe four hours on a Sunday afternoon, totally absorbed in it, hardly moving a muscle. Well, until I get distracted by wanting to look up the exact topography of the Mississippi River in 1863, but that's just my brain wandering, not my body needing to get up and run around, you know?\n\nSo, no, I don't get antsy or feel like I have a motor running inside me. I'm definitely not the kind of guy who needs to go for a jog or hit the gym just to burn off excess energy. Honestly, some of the physical education teachers at my school are always trying to get me to join their after-school faculty basketball league on Thursdays. And I'm always telling them, \"Guys, I've been standing in front of restless sophomores since 7:30 this morning, the absolute last thing I want to do is run up and down a hardwood court.\" I would much, much rather just sit quietly with a book or put on a documentary and just veg out. So yeah, being physically restless or needing to be constantly on the go just isn't something I deal with at all. I'm very good at being still.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, careless mistakes? No. No, I wouldn't say that at all, actually. I mean, I know I was just talking about how my desk gets buried under papers and I abandon my planners, but when it comes to the actual work itself? The grading and the lesson planning? I am incredibly meticulous. I don't just rush through things and leave out details or make silly errors. If anything, I probably focus on the details way too much. 

Like, okay, when I'm grading their Document-Based Questions—DBQs, we call them—for my history classes. It's a very rigid, specific rubric. You have to check for a clear thesis, contextualization, evidence from the provided documents, outside evidence... it's a whole big checklist. And I don't just breeze through it and slap a grade on the top. I sit down at my dining room table—which is this big, heavy antique oak table I actually picked up at an estate sale a few years ago for an absolute steal, I had to rent a U-Haul just to get it to my apartment—and I lay out all the essays in alphabetical order. 

I use these specific purple felt-tip pens, too. A few years ago, the administration had this big professional development seminar where they told us that grading in red ink is too aggressive and psychologically discourages the students. Which, honestly, is a whole other debate about whether we're coddling teenagers too much these days, but whatever, I use the purple pens now. And I will literally read through every single sentence of a student's essay, underlining their arguments, leaving detailed comments in the margins... I never just skip over things or forget to grade a section. 

And then, when I'm putting the grades into the computer? I double-check, sometimes triple-check, the math when I'm tallying up the rubric scores. Because, let me tell you, if you make a careless error and accidentally type a 72 instead of a 92 into the online grading portal, the parents are emailing you within five minutes. They have the district grading app on their phones and it sends them push notifications the absolute second a grade goes live. It is a total nightmare to deal with. So I am terrified of making a data entry mistake like that. I look at the score on the paper, I look at the screen, I type it in, I look back at the paper to verify... I never just forget what I'm doing or make a careless typo. I'm very, very careful about my actual work product. So, yeah, no, making careless errors or missing important details really isn't something I do. I'm almost paranoid about making sure every little thing is exactly right.

Just the focus stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel the need to be active and have trouble engaging in quiet activities?\n\nPatient: Um, feel the need to be active? Trouble with quiet activities? No, not really. Actually, I'd say I'm perfectly fine just sitting still. \n\nI mean, teaching teenagers all day is incredibly exhausting physically. You're constantly on your feet, pacing the front of the room, writing on the board, walking up and down the aisles to make sure nobody is texting under their desks... by the time the final bell rings at 3:15, I am completely wiped out. The grading and the lesson planning? I am incredibly meticulous. I don't just rush through things and leave out details or make silly errors. And I don't just breeze through it and slap a grade on the top. I sit down at my dining room table—which is this big, heavy antique oak table I actually picked up at an estate sale a few years ago for an absolute steal, I had to rent a U-Haul just to get it to my apartment—and I lay out all the essays in alphabetical order. \n\nI use these specific purple felt-tip pens, too. They have the district grading app on their phones and it sends them push notifications the absolute second a grade goes live. It is a total nightmare to deal with. So I am terrified of making a data entry mistake like that. I look at the score on the paper, I look at the screen, I type it in, I look back at the paper to verify... I never just forget what I'm doing or make a careless typo. I'm very, very careful about my actual work product. But it's just normal teacher stress, you know? It's circumstantial. It's not a clinical condition. I've actually never even seen a psychiatrist or a therapist before coming here to talk to you. \n\nSo, yeah, no other diagnoses at all. It's really just always been this... this weird, frustrating struggle with getting sidetracked and being disorganized. And for the longest time, I didn't even think of it as a medical or psychological thing that *could* be diagnosed. I just thought, kind of like what I was saying about my dad calling me a procrastinator, that it was just a personal failing. I thought I just needed to try harder or buy a better planner. \n\nActually, I have an entire drawer in my desk at home just full of old planners. Every single August, right before the school year starts, I go to Staples and I spend like thirty dollars on a really nice, heavy-duty academic planner. I get the nice pens, the color-coded tabs, the whole nine yards, and I tell myself that *this* is the year I'm going to be perfectly organized. And I'll use it religiously for about two weeks, and then I'll forget it in my car one day, and by October it's buried under a pile of ungraded quizzes and I never look at it again. So I always just assumed I was bad at planning, not that I had a condition. So to answer your question, no, I have a completely clean slate medically speaking. Just the focus stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel the need to be active and have trouble engaging in quiet activities?\n\nPatient: Um, feel the need to be active? Trouble with quiet activities? No, not really. Actually, I'd say I'm perfectly fine just sitting still. \n\nI mean, teaching teenagers all day is incredibly exhausting physically. You're constantly on your feet, pacing the front of the room, writing on the board, walking up and down the aisles to make sure nobody is texting under their desks... by the time the final bell rings at 3:15, I am completely wiped out. So when I get home, I honestly just want to sit. I have this big, oversized leather recliner in my living room—it's actually a hand-me-down from my Uncle Pete, and the mechanism is a little squeaky when you pull the lever on the side, but it is just incredibly comfortable. I can sit in that chair for literally hours and not feel any urge whatsoever to get up and move around. \n\nI actually really love quiet activities. I read a lot. Being a history teacher, I tend to gravitate toward these massive, dense biographies. Like, right now, I’m in the middle of this 800-page book on Ulysses S. Grant. It goes into this excruciating detail about his logistics strategies and supply lines during the Vicksburg campaign, which, honestly, most people would probably find incredibly boring and dry. But I can just sit there in total silence for three, maybe four hours on a Sunday afternoon, totally absorbed in it, hardly moving a muscle. Well, until I get distracted by wanting to look up the exact topography of the Mississippi River in 1863, but that's just my brain wandering, not my body needing to get up and run around, you know?\n\nSo, no, I don't get antsy or feel like I have a motor running inside me. I'm definitely not the kind of guy who needs to go for a jog or hit the gym just to burn off excess energy. Honestly, some of the physical education teachers at my school are always trying to get me to join their after-school faculty basketball league on Thursdays. And I'm always telling them, \"Guys, I've been standing in front of restless sophomores since 7:30 this morning, the absolute last thing I want to do is run up and down a hardwood court.\" I would much, much rather just sit quietly with a book or put on a documentary and just veg out. So yeah, being physically restless or needing to be constantly on the go just isn't something I deal with at all. I'm very good at being still.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you make careless mistakes on tasks or activities, such as forgetting to include important details or making errors in your work?\n\nPatient: Um, careless mistakes? No. No, I wouldn't say that at all, actually. I mean, I know I was just talking about how my desk gets buried under papers and I abandon my planners, but when it comes to the actual work itself? The grading and the lesson planning? I am incredibly meticulous. I don't just rush through things and leave out details or make silly errors. If anything, I probably focus on the details way too much. \n\nLike, okay, when I'm grading their Document-Based Questions—DBQs, we call them—for my history classes. It's a very rigid, specific rubric. You have to check for a clear thesis, contextualization, evidence from the provided documents, outside evidence... it's a whole big checklist. And I don't just breeze through it and slap a grade on the top. I sit down at my dining room table—which is this big, heavy antique oak table I actually picked up at an estate sale a few years ago for an absolute steal, I had to rent a U-Haul just to get it to my apartment—and I lay out all the essays in alphabetical order. \n\nI use these specific purple felt-tip pens, too. A few years ago, the administration had this big professional development seminar where they told us that grading in red ink is too aggressive and psychologically discourages the students. Which, honestly, is a whole other debate about whether we're coddling teenagers too much these days, but whatever, I use the purple pens now. And I will literally read through every single sentence of a student's essay, underlining their arguments, leaving detailed comments in the margins... I never just skip over things or forget to grade a section. \n\nAnd then, when I'm putting the grades into the computer? I double-check, sometimes triple-check, the math when I'm tallying up the rubric scores. Because, let me tell you, if you make a careless error and accidentally type a 72 instead of a 92 into the online grading portal, the parents are emailing you within five minutes. They have the district grading app on their phones and it sends them push notifications the absolute second a grade goes live. It is a total nightmare to deal with. So I am terrified of making a data entry mistake like that. I look at the score on the paper, I look at the screen, I type it in, I look back at the paper to verify... I never just forget what I'm doing or make a careless typo. I'm very, very careful about my actual work product. So, yeah, no, making careless errors or missing important details really isn't something I do. I'm almost paranoid about making sure every little thing is exactly right.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, well, nobody ever explicitly used that specific term with me, you know? Like, they didn't sit me down and say the actual letters or suggest it was a clinical thing. But the actual behaviors? Oh, absolutely. People have been pointing those out literally my entire life. 

I mean, if you go all the way back to primary school—my mom actually kept all my old report cards in this big plastic storage bin in her attic. She's very sentimental like that, she keeps every little drawing and macaroni necklace I ever made. Anyway, a couple of Thanksgivings ago we were looking through that bin, and literally every single report card from, like, second grade all the way through fifth, had the exact same comments written in the margins by the teachers. It was almost comical. It was always some variation of, “he is a very bright boy but careless,” or, “he doesn't finish his work unless closely supervised.” 

Which, looking back, must have been so incredibly frustrating for them. I remember my third-grade teacher, Mrs. Gable, she had this whole elaborate system with a sticker chart on the wall, and I almost never got a sticker because I just wouldn't finish the in-class assignments unless she practically stood over my shoulder and tapped her finger on my desk until I got to the last question. 

And my parents, oh man, they were always getting on my case about it at home, too. Because it wasn't just doing the work, it was actually turning it in. My parents would go to check my backpack—I had this heavy-duty blue nylon backpack that I dragged everywhere—and the bottom of it was just an absolute disaster zone. It was like a black hole. They would dig through it and they were always finding these completely crumpled, half-torn worksheets just stuffed at the very bottom, shoved underneath my gym clothes and old permission slips. And the most frustrating part for them was that a lot of the time, they were assignments that I had actually finished, or at least mostly finished, but I just completely forgot to take them out of the bag and put them in the tray on the teacher's desk. 

So my parents were constantly asking me, “Why are you doing this? Why can't you just hand the paper in?” They just thought I was being lazy or that I wasn't applying myself. So, yeah, even though nobody was suggesting a medical diagnosis back then, my teachers and my parents were definitely noticing all those exact same struggles. They just thought I was a smart kid who needed to get his act together.

Which, honestly, is a whole other debate about whether we're coddling teenagers too much these days, but whatever, I use the purple pens now. And I will literally read through every single sentence of a student's essay, underlining their arguments, leaving detailed comments in the margins... I never just skip over things or forget to grade a section. \n\nAnd then, when I'm putting the grades into the computer? I double-check, sometimes triple-check, the math when I'm tallying up the rubric scores. Because, let me tell you, if you make a careless error and accidentally type a 72 instead of a 92 into the online grading portal, the parents are emailing you within five minutes. They have the district grading app on their phones and it sends them push notifications the absolute second a grade goes live. It is a total nightmare to deal with. So I am terrified of making a data entry mistake like that. I look at the score on the paper, I look at the screen, I type it in, I look back at the paper to verify... I never just forget what I'm doing or make a careless typo. I'm very, very careful about my actual work product. So, yeah, no, making careless errors or missing important details really isn't something I do. I'm almost paranoid about making sure every little thing is exactly right.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been told by a teacher, family member, or friend that you have symptoms similar to those of ADHD?\n\nPatient: Um, well, nobody ever explicitly used that specific term with me, you know? Like, they didn't sit me down and say the actual letters or suggest it was a clinical thing. But the actual behaviors? Oh, absolutely. People have been pointing those out literally my entire life. \n\nI mean, if you go all the way back to primary school—my mom actually kept all my old report cards in this big plastic storage bin in her attic. She's very sentimental like that, she keeps every little drawing and macaroni necklace I ever made. It was almost comical. It was always some variation of, “he is a very bright boy but careless,” or, “he doesn't finish his work unless closely supervised.” \n\nWhich, looking back, must have been so incredibly frustrating for them. I remember my third-grade teacher, Mrs. Gable, she had this whole elaborate system with a sticker chart on the wall, and I almost never got a sticker because I just wouldn't finish the in-class assignments unless she practically stood over my shoulder and tapped her finger on my desk until I got to the last question. \n\nAnd my parents, oh man, they were always getting on my case about it at home, too. Because it wasn't just doing the work, it was actually turning it in. My parents would go to check my backpack—I had this heavy-duty blue nylon backpack that I dragged everywhere—and the bottom of it was just an absolute disaster zone. It was like a black hole. They would dig through it and they were always finding these completely crumpled, half-torn worksheets just stuffed at the very bottom, shoved underneath my gym clothes and old permission slips. Why can't you just hand the paper in?” They just thought I was being lazy or that I wasn't applying myself. So, yeah, even though nobody was suggesting a medical diagnosis back then, my teachers and my parents were definitely noticing all those exact same struggles. They just thought I was a smart kid who needed to get his act together.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. You're constantly on your feet, pacing the front of the room, writing on the board, walking up and down the aisles to make sure nobody is texting under their desks... by the time the final bell rings at 3:15, I am completely wiped out. So when I get home, I honestly just want to sit. I have this big, oversized leather recliner in my living room—it's actually a hand-me-down from my Uncle Pete, and the mechanism is a little squeaky when you pull the lever on the side, but it is just incredibly comfortable. I can sit in that chair for literally hours and not feel any urge whatsoever to get up and move around. \n\nI actually really love quiet activities. I read a lot. Being a history teacher, I tend to gravitate toward these massive, dense biographies. Like, right now, I’m in the middle of this 800-page book on Ulysses S. Grant. It goes into this excruciating detail about his logistics strategies and supply lines during the Vicksburg campaign, which, honestly, most people would probably find incredibly boring and dry. But I can just sit there in total silence for three, maybe four hours on a Sunday afternoon, totally absorbed in it, hardly moving a muscle. Well, until I get distracted by wanting to look up the exact topography of the Mississippi River in 1863, but that's just my brain wandering, not my body needing to get up and run around, you know?\n\nSo, no, I don't get antsy or feel like I have a motor running inside me. I'm definitely not the kind of guy who needs to go for a jog or hit the gym just to burn off excess energy. Honestly, some of the physical education teachers at my school are always trying to get me to join their after-school faculty basketball league on Thursdays. And I'm always telling them, \"Guys, I've been standing in front of restless sophomores since 7:30 this morning, the absolute last thing I want to do is run up and down a hardwood court.\" I would much, much rather just sit quietly with a book or put on a documentary and just veg out. So yeah, being physically restless or needing to be constantly on the go just isn't something I deal with at all. I'm very good at being still.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you make careless mistakes on tasks or activities, such as forgetting to include important details or making errors in your work?\n\nPatient: Um, careless mistakes? No. No, I wouldn't say that at all, actually. I mean, I know I was just talking about how my desk gets buried under papers and I abandon my planners, but when it comes to the actual work itself? The grading and the lesson planning? I am incredibly meticulous. I don't just rush through things and leave out details or make silly errors. If anything, I probably focus on the details way too much. \n\nLike, okay, when I'm grading their Document-Based Questions—DBQs, we call them—for my history classes. It's a very rigid, specific rubric. You have to check for a clear thesis, contextualization, evidence from the provided documents, outside evidence... it's a whole big checklist. And I don't just breeze through it and slap a grade on the top. I sit down at my dining room table—which is this big, heavy antique oak table I actually picked up at an estate sale a few years ago for an absolute steal, I had to rent a U-Haul just to get it to my apartment—and I lay out all the essays in alphabetical order. \n\nI use these specific purple felt-tip pens, too. A few years ago, the administration had this big professional development seminar where they told us that grading in red ink is too aggressive and psychologically discourages the students. Which, honestly, is a whole other debate about whether we're coddling teenagers too much these days, but whatever, I use the purple pens now. And I will literally read through every single sentence of a student's essay, underlining their arguments, leaving detailed comments in the margins... I never just skip over things or forget to grade a section. \n\nAnd then, when I'm putting the grades into the computer? I double-check, sometimes triple-check, the math when I'm tallying up the rubric scores. Because, let me tell you, if you make a careless error and accidentally type a 72 instead of a 92 into the online grading portal, the parents are emailing you within five minutes. They have the district grading app on their phones and it sends them push notifications the absolute second a grade goes live. It is a total nightmare to deal with. So I am terrified of making a data entry mistake like that. I look at the score on the paper, I look at the screen, I type it in, I look back at the paper to verify... I never just forget what I'm doing or make a careless typo. I'm very, very careful about my actual work product. So, yeah, no, making careless errors or missing important details really isn't something I do. I'm almost paranoid about making sure every little thing is exactly right.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been told by a teacher, family member, or friend that you have symptoms similar to those of ADHD?\n\nPatient: Um, well, nobody ever explicitly used that specific term with me, you know? Like, they didn't sit me down and say the actual letters or suggest it was a clinical thing. But the actual behaviors? Oh, absolutely. People have been pointing those out literally my entire life. \n\nI mean, if you go all the way back to primary school—my mom actually kept all my old report cards in this big plastic storage bin in her attic. She's very sentimental like that, she keeps every little drawing and macaroni necklace I ever made. Anyway, a couple of Thanksgivings ago we were looking through that bin, and literally every single report card from, like, second grade all the way through fifth, had the exact same comments written in the margins by the teachers. It was almost comical. It was always some variation of, “he is a very bright boy but careless,” or, “he doesn't finish his work unless closely supervised.” \n\nWhich, looking back, must have been so incredibly frustrating for them. I remember my third-grade teacher, Mrs. Gable, she had this whole elaborate system with a sticker chart on the wall, and I almost never got a sticker because I just wouldn't finish the in-class assignments unless she practically stood over my shoulder and tapped her finger on my desk until I got to the last question. \n\nAnd my parents, oh man, they were always getting on my case about it at home, too. Because it wasn't just doing the work, it was actually turning it in. My parents would go to check my backpack—I had this heavy-duty blue nylon backpack that I dragged everywhere—and the bottom of it was just an absolute disaster zone. It was like a black hole. They would dig through it and they were always finding these completely crumpled, half-torn worksheets just stuffed at the very bottom, shoved underneath my gym clothes and old permission slips. And the most frustrating part for them was that a lot of the time, they were assignments that I had actually finished, or at least mostly finished, but I just completely forgot to take them out of the bag and put them in the tray on the teacher's desk. \n\nSo my parents were constantly asking me, “Why are you doing this? Why can't you just hand the paper in?” They just thought I was being lazy or that I wasn't applying myself. So, yeah, even though nobody was suggesting a medical diagnosis back then, my teachers and my parents were definitely noticing all those exact same struggles. They just thought I was a smart kid who needed to get his act together.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

Um, definitely the mind wandering. Like I was saying earlier about the recliner and not wanting to play basketball, I really don't get that physical restlessness at all, and I wouldn't say I'm impulsive either. I'm a pretty cautious guy, actually. So it's—it's 100% the focus and the mental drift. 

In fact, that's exactly why I came in to see you. Because it’s getting to the point where it’s just incredibly frustrating. I can sit down at that big oak dining table I told you about, and I'll have a huge stack of essays in front of me—maybe the ones on the Industrial Revolution we just wrapped up—or one of those massive, dry curriculum documents the district office sends out every quarter that we're required to review and sign off on. And I will sit there, purple pen in hand, fully intending to work. I'll even tell myself, "Okay, you are going to knock out fifteen of these right now, no distractions." 

But then... I don't know, within ten minutes, I’ll suddenly snap back to reality and realize I’ve just been staring at the exact same page the entire time. Like, I haven't even flipped to the second page of the essay. My eyes are physically looking at the paper, but my brain is completely gone. I’ll be thinking about three other things—like whether I remembered to switch the laundry to the dryer this morning, or a podcast I listened to on my commute about the Cold War, or how I need to get my car's oil changed before the weekend—and I am barely taking in what’s actually in front of me. 

I'll read the same sentence written by a sophomore four, maybe five times, and still have absolutely no idea what they're trying to argue. And then I have to shake my head and start all over again. So, yeah, I don't need to get up and run around, but keeping my brain locked onto the task at hand? It's like trying to hold water in my hands. The focus is definitely the main issue.

And I will literally read through every single sentence of a student's essay, underlining their arguments, leaving detailed comments in the margins... I never just skip over things or forget to grade a section. \n\nAnd then, when I'm putting the grades into the computer? I double-check, sometimes triple-check, the math when I'm tallying up the rubric scores. Because, let me tell you, if you make a careless error and accidentally type a 72 instead of a 92 into the online grading portal, the parents are emailing you within five minutes. They have the district grading app on their phones and it sends them push notifications the absolute second a grade goes live. It is a total nightmare to deal with. So I am terrified of making a data entry mistake like that. I look at the score on the paper, I look at the screen, I type it in, I look back at the paper to verify... I never just forget what I'm doing or make a careless typo. I'm very, very careful about my actual work product. So, yeah, no, making careless errors or missing important details really isn't something I do. I'm almost paranoid about making sure every little thing is exactly right.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been told by a teacher, family member, or friend that you have symptoms similar to those of ADHD?\n\nPatient: Um, well, nobody ever explicitly used that specific term with me, you know? People have been pointing those out literally my entire life. \n\nI mean, if you go all the way back to primary school—my mom actually kept all my old report cards in this big plastic storage bin in her attic. She's very sentimental like that, she keeps every little drawing and macaroni necklace I ever made. Anyway, a couple of Thanksgivings ago we were looking through that bin, and literally every single report card from, like, second grade all the way through fifth, had the exact same comments written in the margins by the teachers. It was almost comical. It was always some variation of, “he is a very bright boy but careless,” or, “he doesn't finish his work unless closely supervised.” \n\nWhich, looking back, must have been so incredibly frustrating for them. I remember my third-grade teacher, Mrs. Gable, she had this whole elaborate system with a sticker chart on the wall, and I almost never got a sticker because I just wouldn't finish the in-class assignments unless she practically stood over my shoulder and tapped her finger on my desk until I got to the last question. \n\nAnd my parents, oh man, they were always getting on my case about it at home, too. Because it wasn't just doing the work, it was actually turning it in. My parents would go to check my backpack—I had this heavy-duty blue nylon backpack that I dragged everywhere—and the bottom of it was just an absolute disaster zone. It was like a black hole. They would dig through it and they were always finding these completely crumpled, half-torn worksheets just stuffed at the very bottom, shoved underneath my gym clothes and old permission slips. And the most frustrating part for them was that a lot of the time, they were assignments that I had actually finished, or at least mostly finished, but I just completely forgot to take them out of the bag and put them in the tray on the teacher's desk. \n\nSo my parents were constantly asking me, “Why are you doing this? Why can't you just hand the paper in?” They just thought I was being lazy or that I wasn't applying myself. So, yeah, even though nobody was suggesting a medical diagnosis back then, my teachers and my parents were definitely noticing all those exact same struggles. They just thought I was a smart kid who needed to get his act together.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your mind wandering and difficulty with focus are your main issues, or do you also struggle with feeling restless or impulsive at times?\n\nPatient: Um, definitely the mind wandering. I'm a pretty cautious guy, actually. So it's—it's 100% the focus and the mental drift. \n\nIn fact, that's exactly why I came in to see you. Because it’s getting to the point where it’s just incredibly frustrating. And I will sit there, purple pen in hand, fully intending to work. I'll even tell myself, \"Okay, you are going to knock out fifteen of these right now, no distractions.\" \n\nBut then... It's like trying to hold water in my hands. The focus is definitely the main issue.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. They'll be like, \"Hey, we're getting Thai food from that place down the street, what do you want?\" And I will literally sit there staring at the menu for twenty minutes trying to decide if I want Pad Thai or Green Curry, thinking about if the curry will be too heavy and make me sleepy during my 6th-period class, or if the Pad Thai is going to be too messy to eat while I'm trying to grade quizzes. I just don't have that spontaneous, jump-right-in gene. I really don't do anything without completely over-analyzing it first, so acting without thinking is definitely not an issue for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with any other medical or psychological conditions that might be contributing to your difficulties with focus and organization?\n\nPatient: Um, other medical or psychological conditions? No, no, definitely not. I mean, I haven't really been to the doctor much in my life anyway, aside from just the standard stuff. \n\nLike, I go for my routine physicals every year, mostly because our teachers' union negotiated this really good health insurance plan a few years back—which, honestly, was a whole big ordeal with the district, they almost went on strike over the premiums, but anyway, we get these free annual wellness checks now. So I go to my primary care guy, Dr. Evans, and he always says I'm perfectly healthy. He does the whole workup, checks my blood pressure, listens to my heart, and it's always completely fine. I've never had any major health issues, no surgeries, no chronic illnesses or anything like that. I don't have, like, thyroid problems or anything physical that I know of that would mess with my focus. I mean, I guess I don't sleep great sometimes, but that's just because I'm up late trying to finish grading the papers I got distracted from earlier, you know? It's not because of a medical sleep condition. \n\nAnd as for the psychological side of things, no, nothing there either. I've never been diagnosed with depression or anxiety or anything like that. I mean, I get stressed out, sure. Teaching sophomores is just inherently stressful. Especially around standardized testing time in the spring, the whole building just turns into this massive pressure cooker, and I definitely feel the stress then. But it's just normal teacher stress, you know? It's circumstantial. It's not a clinical condition. I've actually never even seen a psychiatrist or a therapist before coming here to talk to you. \n\nSo, yeah, no other diagnoses at all. It's really just always been this... this weird, frustrating struggle with getting sidetracked and being disorganized. And for the longest time, I didn't even think of it as a medical or psychological thing that *could* be diagnosed. I just thought, kind of like what I was saying about my dad calling me a procrastinator, that it was just a personal failing. I thought I just needed to try harder or buy a better planner. \n\nActually, I have an entire drawer in my desk at home just full of old planners. Every single August, right before the school year starts, I go to Staples and I spend like thirty dollars on a really nice, heavy-duty academic planner. I get the nice pens, the color-coded tabs, the whole nine yards, and I tell myself that *this* is the year I'm going to be perfectly organized. And I'll use it religiously for about two weeks, and then I'll forget it in my car one day, and by October it's buried under a pile of ungraded quizzes and I never look at it again. So I always just assumed I was bad at planning, not that I had a condition. So to answer your question, no, I have a completely clean slate medically speaking. Just the focus stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel the need to be active and have trouble engaging in quiet activities?\n\nPatient: Um, feel the need to be active? Trouble with quiet activities? No, not really. Actually, I'd say I'm perfectly fine just sitting still. \n\nI mean, teaching teenagers all day is incredibly exhausting physically. You're constantly on your feet, pacing the front of the room, writing on the board, walking up and down the aisles to make sure nobody is texting under their desks... by the time the final bell rings at 3:15, I am completely wiped out. So when I get home, I honestly just want to sit. I have this big, oversized leather recliner in my living room—it's actually a hand-me-down from my Uncle Pete, and the mechanism is a little squeaky when you pull the lever on the side, but it is just incredibly comfortable. I can sit in that chair for literally hours and not feel any urge whatsoever to get up and move around. \n\nI actually really love quiet activities. I read a lot. Being a history teacher, I tend to gravitate toward these massive, dense biographies. Like, right now, I’m in the middle of this 800-page book on Ulysses S. Grant. It goes into this excruciating detail about his logistics strategies and supply lines during the Vicksburg campaign, which, honestly, most people would probably find incredibly boring and dry. But I can just sit there in total silence for three, maybe four hours on a Sunday afternoon, totally absorbed in it, hardly moving a muscle. Well, until I get distracted by wanting to look up the exact topography of the Mississippi River in 1863, but that's just my brain wandering, not my body needing to get up and run around, you know?\n\nSo, no, I don't get antsy or feel like I have a motor running inside me. I'm definitely not the kind of guy who needs to go for a jog or hit the gym just to burn off excess energy. Honestly, some of the physical education teachers at my school are always trying to get me to join their after-school faculty basketball league on Thursdays. And I'm always telling them, \"Guys, I've been standing in front of restless sophomores since 7:30 this morning, the absolute last thing I want to do is run up and down a hardwood court.\" I would much, much rather just sit quietly with a book or put on a documentary and just veg out. So yeah, being physically restless or needing to be constantly on the go just isn't something I deal with at all. I'm very good at being still.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you make careless mistakes on tasks or activities, such as forgetting to include important details or making errors in your work?\n\nPatient: Um, careless mistakes? No. No, I wouldn't say that at all, actually. I mean, I know I was just talking about how my desk gets buried under papers and I abandon my planners, but when it comes to the actual work itself? The grading and the lesson planning? I am incredibly meticulous. I don't just rush through things and leave out details or make silly errors. If anything, I probably focus on the details way too much. \n\nLike, okay, when I'm grading their Document-Based Questions—DBQs, we call them—for my history classes. It's a very rigid, specific rubric. You have to check for a clear thesis, contextualization, evidence from the provided documents, outside evidence... it's a whole big checklist. And I don't just breeze through it and slap a grade on the top. I sit down at my dining room table—which is this big, heavy antique oak table I actually picked up at an estate sale a few years ago for an absolute steal, I had to rent a U-Haul just to get it to my apartment—and I lay out all the essays in alphabetical order. \n\nI use these specific purple felt-tip pens, too. A few years ago, the administration had this big professional development seminar where they told us that grading in red ink is too aggressive and psychologically discourages the students. Which, honestly, is a whole other debate about whether we're coddling teenagers too much these days, but whatever, I use the purple pens now. And I will literally read through every single sentence of a student's essay, underlining their arguments, leaving detailed comments in the margins... I never just skip over things or forget to grade a section. \n\nAnd then, when I'm putting the grades into the computer? I double-check, sometimes triple-check, the math when I'm tallying up the rubric scores. Because, let me tell you, if you make a careless error and accidentally type a 72 instead of a 92 into the online grading portal, the parents are emailing you within five minutes. They have the district grading app on their phones and it sends them push notifications the absolute second a grade goes live. It is a total nightmare to deal with. So I am terrified of making a data entry mistake like that. I look at the score on the paper, I look at the screen, I type it in, I look back at the paper to verify... I never just forget what I'm doing or make a careless typo. I'm very, very careful about my actual work product. So, yeah, no, making careless errors or missing important details really isn't something I do. I'm almost paranoid about making sure every little thing is exactly right.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been told by a teacher, family member, or friend that you have symptoms similar to those of ADHD?\n\nPatient: Um, well, nobody ever explicitly used that specific term with me, you know? Like, they didn't sit me down and say the actual letters or suggest it was a clinical thing. But the actual behaviors? Oh, absolutely. People have been pointing those out literally my entire life. \n\nI mean, if you go all the way back to primary school—my mom actually kept all my old report cards in this big plastic storage bin in her attic. She's very sentimental like that, she keeps every little drawing and macaroni necklace I ever made. Anyway, a couple of Thanksgivings ago we were looking through that bin, and literally every single report card from, like, second grade all the way through fifth, had the exact same comments written in the margins by the teachers. It was almost comical. It was always some variation of, “he is a very bright boy but careless,” or, “he doesn't finish his work unless closely supervised.” \n\nWhich, looking back, must have been so incredibly frustrating for them. I remember my third-grade teacher, Mrs. Gable, she had this whole elaborate system with a sticker chart on the wall, and I almost never got a sticker because I just wouldn't finish the in-class assignments unless she practically stood over my shoulder and tapped her finger on my desk until I got to the last question. \n\nAnd my parents, oh man, they were always getting on my case about it at home, too. Because it wasn't just doing the work, it was actually turning it in. My parents would go to check my backpack—I had this heavy-duty blue nylon backpack that I dragged everywhere—and the bottom of it was just an absolute disaster zone. It was like a black hole. They would dig through it and they were always finding these completely crumpled, half-torn worksheets just stuffed at the very bottom, shoved underneath my gym clothes and old permission slips. And the most frustrating part for them was that a lot of the time, they were assignments that I had actually finished, or at least mostly finished, but I just completely forgot to take them out of the bag and put them in the tray on the teacher's desk. \n\nSo my parents were constantly asking me, “Why are you doing this? Why can't you just hand the paper in?” They just thought I was being lazy or that I wasn't applying myself. So, yeah, even though nobody was suggesting a medical diagnosis back then, my teachers and my parents were definitely noticing all those exact same struggles. They just thought I was a smart kid who needed to get his act together.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your mind wandering and difficulty with focus are your main issues, or do you also struggle with feeling restless or impulsive at times?\n\nPatient: Um, definitely the mind wandering. Like I was saying earlier about the recliner and not wanting to play basketball, I really don't get that physical restlessness at all, and I wouldn't say I'm impulsive either. I'm a pretty cautious guy, actually. So it's—it's 100% the focus and the mental drift. \n\nIn fact, that's exactly why I came in to see you. Because it’s getting to the point where it’s just incredibly frustrating. I can sit down at that big oak dining table I told you about, and I'll have a huge stack of essays in front of me—maybe the ones on the Industrial Revolution we just wrapped up—or one of those massive, dry curriculum documents the district office sends out every quarter that we're required to review and sign off on. And I will sit there, purple pen in hand, fully intending to work. I'll even tell myself, \"Okay, you are going to knock out fifteen of these right now, no distractions.\" \n\nBut then... I don't know, within ten minutes, I’ll suddenly snap back to reality and realize I’ve just been staring at the exact same page the entire time. Like, I haven't even flipped to the second page of the essay. My eyes are physically looking at the paper, but my brain is completely gone. I’ll be thinking about three other things—like whether I remembered to switch the laundry to the dryer this morning, or a podcast I listened to on my commute about the Cold War, or how I need to get my car's oil changed before the weekend—and I am barely taking in what’s actually in front of me. \n\nI'll read the same sentence written by a sophomore four, maybe five times, and still have absolutely no idea what they're trying to argue. And then I have to shake my head and start all over again. So, yeah, I don't need to get up and run around, but keeping my brain locked onto the task at hand? It's like trying to hold water in my hands. The focus is definitely the main issue."
  }
